I don't get excited about a good meal anymore. I'm just... eating because I have to, just like I'm doing everything else. Just moving through obligations.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I was actually just saying that a minute ago. It's exactly why I finally came in to see you. The part of me that used to reach out for people, or enjoy my work, or yoga... \n\nI used to have this really wonderful yoga mat, actually. It was made of recycled cork. They say cork is naturally antimicrobial, which is nice, but honestly, it always smelled a little bit like a wine cork. My neighbor loves wine, she actually went to that new vineyard they opened out on Route 9 last weekend. She said the traffic to get out there was absolutely horrendous, though. She sat on the highway for two hours. She drives one of those massive SUVs, which just seems so impractical for living in the city. I don't know how she ever finds street parking. I mean, with those new app meters I was talking about earlier, it's hard enough to park a compact car, let alone a tank like that...\n\nUm, sorry. I lost my place again. You asked about losing interest in things. Yes. Like I said earlier, it's all just gone very quiet. The yoga, the journaling, the galleries... I don't enjoy any of it. I'm mostly just moving through obligations at this point.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Um, let me think. It's been about eight months. Yeah, eight months. It started right around the time we took my older relative into our apartment. We had to completely clear out the second bedroom for them, which was a huge project because I was using it to store all my old social work textbooks from grad school. \n\nThey are so incredibly heavy, those books. I tried to donate them to the local library, but the librarian told me they don't accept textbooks anymore because the information gets outdated too quickly. Which makes sense, I guess, but it just feels like such a terrible waste to throw out perfectly good hardcover books. I mean, the amount of paper that goes into landfills every year is staggering. I actually started looking into composting our paper towels and junk mail to do my part, but you have to be really careful about the glossy grocery store inserts because of the toxic ink. My neighbor—not the one with the SUV, the other one—tried composting and ended up with a yard full of raccoons. They are surprisingly smart animals, raccoons. They figured out how to unlatch her supposedly animal-proof bins in just two nights...\n\nSorry, I'm rambling again. You asked how long. Right. So, about eight months. That's how long this whole change in how I'm thinking, and working, and connecting with people has been going on. Just... ever since they moved in.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Weight? Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, my clothes still fit the same as they always have. I haven't really noticed any big changes on the scale or anything. I actually had to buy a new scale a few months ago because the battery cover on my old one broke and the batteries kept falling out. \n\nIt's so frustrating how cheaply things are made now. Everything is just plastic that snaps if you look at it wrong. I tried to tape the cover back on, but it was that really cheap, flimsy tape I got at the dollar store. Have you noticed how dollar stores aren't even really a dollar anymore? They changed the name of the one down the street to like, \"Dollar and Quarter Tree\" or something. It's just inflation, I know, but it feels a little deceptive. My husband used to love going in there just to see what weird off-brand snacks they had. He once found these chips that were supposed to taste like a cheeseburger, and they actually smelled exactly like pickles. It was the strangest thing. I can't stand the smell of pickles, honestly, they just... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, weight. No, I haven't lost any weight. Even with not enjoying food, I guess I'm still eating enough to maintain where I'm at. Just... eating whatever's around.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Moving more slowly? Um, no, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed feeling physically slower or anything. I mean, my morning walk to the subway takes the exact same amount of time it always has. I time it sometimes because I like to listen to this one specific podcast on the way, and the episodes are exactly twenty-two minutes long. \n\nIt's a podcast about historical mysteries, actually. The host has this really soothing voice, almost like a late-night radio DJ. I used to listen to the radio all the time when I was younger, especially when I was driving back and forth to my first social work internship. I had this beat-up old Honda Civic, and the tape deck was broken, so I was stuck with whatever was on the FM dial. You know, they just don't make cars like that anymore. That thing ran forever, even though the heat barely worked. I remember driving in January with my winter coat and gloves on, just freezing. The winters here used to be so much worse, don't you think? Now it barely snows before January, but back then we'd have a foot of snow on the ground by Thanksgiving. I always loved Thanksgiving, making the cranberry sauce from scratch... \n\nUm, anyway. Sorry. Moving slowly. No, I haven't noticed that. I'm just kind of going at my normal pace, even if I don't really want to be doing the things I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Worthless or guilty? Um, no, not really. I don't think I'm having those kinds of thoughts. I know what that looks like, obviously, from my clients at the clinic. People carry so much guilt, especially with substance abuse stuff. I had this one woman years ago who felt so incredibly guilty about stealing her roommate's toaster to pawn it. \n\nWhich is such a strange thing to pawn, if you think about it, a toaster. It was one of those really nice retro-looking ones, though, the kind that cost like a hundred dollars at those fancy kitchen stores. I actually love walking through those stores just to look at the gadgets. They have a specific tool for absolutely everything now, like an avocado slicer. Why do you need a special plastic tool to slice an avocado? You just use a knife. My husband bought an avocado slicer once, and it just sat in the kitchen drawer getting jammed every time you tried to open it. The drawer in our kitchen always sticks anyway because the wood swelled up last summer when the humidity was so awful. We tried sanding it down to make it fit better, but the sawdust got absolutely everywhere. It took me three days to get it out of the crevices of the blender. And you know how hard blenders are to clean anyway, with the blades at the bottom...\n\nSorry, I completely lost my train of thought again. Guilt, you said. No, I don't feel worthless or guilty. I'm not really having thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about death?\n\nPatient: Death? Um, no. I don't really think about dying or anything like that. I mean, I see enough of that kind of heavy stuff at work, you know? The clinic is right next to this old cemetery, actually. It's one of those really beautiful, historic ones with the big weeping willows and the elaborate stone angels. \n\nI used to walk through there on my lunch break sometimes, just because it was quiet. It's amazing how peaceful it can be right in the middle of the city. There's this one headstone from the 1800s that always caught my eye because the carving on it was of a little ship. I always wondered if the person was a sailor or if it was just symbolic, you know? Like sailing into the next life. My grandfather was in the Navy, actually. He used to tell these incredible stories about being stationed in the Pacific. He brought back this beautiful carved wooden box that my grandmother kept her sewing supplies in. I still have it. It smells like old thread and cedar. Cedar is such a distinct smell, isn't it? We tried to put cedar blocks in our closets last year to keep the moths away, but I swear the moths just ignored them. I ended up finding holes in my favorite wool sweater anyway. It was this gorgeous forest green color that I bought at a little boutique when we went to Vermont. Vermont is so stunning in the fall...\n\nWait, sorry. Death. No, I don't think about death. I just... I don't really have those kinds of thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've lost interest in almost all activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Lost interest... yeah. I guess that's actually what finally brought me in. It's like the part of me that used to reach out for people and actually take pleasure in things has just gone very quiet. I used to love my work, doing yoga, going to art galleries, even just journaling in the evening. \n\nI have this really beautiful leather-bound journal I bought at this little stationery shop downtown. They sell the most amazing pens there, too. You know, those fountain pens that make you feel like you're writing a letter in the nineteenth century? I bought one with this deep blue ink, almost like a sapphire color. It reminds me of the ocean. My husband and I went to the coast for our honeymoon, and the water was exactly that color. We stayed in this tiny bed and breakfast where the owner made the most incredible blueberry scones every morning. I've tried to recreate that recipe so many times, but they never turn out quite right. I think it's because she used fresh buttermilk from a local farm. You just can't get buttermilk like that at the regular grocery store. The grocery store near us is always so chaotic anyway, especially on Sunday afternoons. People just abandon their carts right in the middle of the aisle, and the wheels always squeak...\n\nUm, but yeah. The yoga and the galleries and all of it. I just don't care about them anymore. I'm mostly just moving through obligations now. It's all just very quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your loss of interest in activities is affecting your daily life a lot?\n\nPatient: Affecting my daily life? Yeah, I suppose it is. I mean, I'm still showing up to work at the clinic every day, but it's... it's not the same. I've actually missed a couple of important grant deadlines recently, which is so unlike me. And my supervisor, she's been so nice about it, but she's quietly moved some of my more demanding clients to other clinicians. She can tell I'm just not functioning at my usual level. \n\nShe's a really observant supervisor, actually. She always notices little things, like if someone changes their hair or gets new glasses. I got new glasses a few months ago, these tortoiseshell frames. I've always loved tortoiseshell. It goes with almost everything, you know? Unlike those bright red frames my sister bought last year. She thought they made her look artsy, but they just clashed with all her clothes. She wears a lot of mustard yellow, which is a really hard color to pull off unless you have the right skin tone. We were trying to find her a dress for our cousin's wedding, and she insisted on this yellow silk thing. The wedding was outdoors in August, and silk is just terrible in the heat. It shows every drop of sweat. Plus, there were mosquitoes everywhere. They had those citronella candles burning, but the smell of citronella always gives me a headache. It's so overpowering. I prefer lavender or maybe eucalyptus. I used to hang fresh eucalyptus in my shower because the steam makes it smell like a spa...\n\nSorry, what was I saying? Oh, right. Work. Yeah, I'm just not functioning the way I used to, so it's definitely affecting things.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have lost interest in almost all activities or is it more like most of them?\n\nPatient: Almost all or most... um, I'm not really sure how to measure the difference, to be honest. I haven't really sat down and counted them up or anything. It just feels like... a lot of them. \n\nLike I mentioned the yoga before. I used to go to this studio over on 4th street, and they had these heated bamboo floors that were just incredible in the winter. The winters here get so brutally cold, don't they? I actually had to buy special snow tires for my Subaru last year because the city plows never seem to make it down our street until noon. They really need to update their plowing routes. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he actually bought his own snowblower just so he could do the sidewalks for the whole block. He's such a sweet older man. He used to be a baker, and sometimes he brings over these little tins of shortbread cookies during the holidays. Shortbread is so rich and buttery, you really need a good cup of Earl Grey tea to go with it to cut through that richness. I have this beautiful ceramic teapot that keeps the tea warm for hours. It has these little hand-painted bluebirds on it... \n\nAnyway, sorry. Um, all or most? Yeah, I really couldn't tell you the exact ratio. Just... the things I used to do, I don't really do them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to find some pleasure in at least a few things, even if it's not as much as you used to?\n\nPatient: Find pleasure in a few things... I don't know. Like I was trying to say earlier, it's really just that the part of me that used to reach out for people and actually take pleasure in things—my work at the clinic, the yoga, the art galleries, my journaling—it's all just gone very quiet. I'm mostly just moving through obligations now. \n\nObligations... there are just so many of them, aren't there? I feel like I'm always writing lists. I have this notepad on my fridge with a magnetic back, but the magnet is really weak, so it's always sliding down and getting caught behind the handle. The fridge is stainless steel, which looks nice, but it shows every single fingerprint. My husband is terrible about grabbing the door with messy hands, especially when he's making his famous chili. He uses three different kinds of beans in it. Black beans, kidney beans, and pinto beans. I always tell him that's too many beans, it messes with the texture, but he swears by it. He won a chili cook-off at his office five years ago and hasn't let anyone forget it. The trophy was this ridiculous plastic chili pepper wearing a cowboy hat. We had it on the mantle for a while, right next to this beautiful antique clock my grandmother left me. She bought that clock in Switzerland when she was traveling through Europe in her twenties. She took a train through the Alps, and she used to tell me stories about how the mountains looked like they were carved out of sugar...\n\nBut, um... pleasure. No, not really. Even the few things, it's just... quiet. Just obligations.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that there's anything at all that you still look forward to or enjoy, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Look forward to? Um... I guess that's really what finally brought me in to see you. I just... I only buy the ones with the dotted pages because the lined ones feel too restrictive, you know? Like you're forced to write in straight lines, but sometimes you want to draw a little sketch or make a mind map. I tried a bullet journal once, but keeping up with the index was exhausting. My sister is obsessed with bullet journaling. She has all these expensive mildliner highlighters in pastel colors. She color-codes everything—her meals, her workouts, her moods. I think she spends more time tracking her life than actually living it. She tried to get me into it and bought me this set of pens for my birthday, but they bleed through the paper. I'm mostly just moving through obligations now. Um, yeah, unable to experience pleasure at all... I mean, I think I've been saying that, right? That's really what finally brought me in here to see you. It's just that the part of me that used to reach out for people, the part that actually took pleasure in things—my work at the clinic, the yoga, the art galleries, even my journaling—it's all just gone very quiet. 

Quiet is such a strange word, isn't it? Like, you think of a quiet room, but even a quiet room has noise. The hum of the refrigerator, or the ticking of a clock, or the sound of your own breathing. I have this humidifier in my bedroom, and it makes this low, constant humming sound. At first, it drove me absolutely crazy. I couldn't sleep for the first three nights I had it. I kept thinking it sounded like a tiny airplane trying to take off from my dresser. But then, after a while, I got so used to it that I actually couldn't sleep without it. When it broke last month—the little motor just burned out, I think because I forgot to clean the filter—I had to go out and buy a new one immediately. I went to three different stores because I wanted the exact same model. The new ones have these bright blue LED lights on them that you can't turn off, which is terrible for your circadian rhythm. You're supposed to sleep in complete darkness, you know. My bedroom has blackout curtains, the really thick velvet ones. They're a deep emerald green, which I read is a very calming color for a bedroom...

But, um, yes. Pleasure. I'm mostly just moving through obligations now. That's really all it is.

I actually started looking into composting our paper towels and junk mail to do my part, but you have to be really careful about the glossy grocery store inserts because of the toxic ink. Everything is just plastic that snaps if you look at it wrong. I tried to tape the cover back on, but it was that really cheap, flimsy tape I got at the dollar store. It's just inflation, I know, but it feels a little deceptive. My husband used to love going in there just to see what weird off-brand snacks they had. Just... eating whatever's around.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Moving more slowly? Um, no, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed feeling physically slower or anything. I mean, my morning walk to the subway takes the exact same amount of time it always has. I time it sometimes because I like to listen to this one specific podcast on the way, and the episodes are exactly twenty-two minutes long. \n\nIt's a podcast about historical mysteries, actually. The host has this really soothing voice, almost like a late-night radio DJ. I used to listen to the radio all the time when I was younger, especially when I was driving back and forth to my first social work internship. I had this beat-up old Honda Civic, and the tape deck was broken, so I was stuck with whatever was on the FM dial. You know, they just don't make cars like that anymore. That thing ran forever, even though the heat barely worked. I remember driving in January with my winter coat and gloves on, just freezing. The winters here used to be so much worse, don't you think? Now it barely snows before January, but back then we'd have a foot of snow on the ground by Thanksgiving. I always loved Thanksgiving, making the cranberry sauce from scratch... \n\nUm, anyway. Sorry. Moving slowly. No, I haven't noticed that. I'm just kind of going at my normal pace, even if I don't really want to be doing the things I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Worthless or guilty? Um, no, not really. I don't think I'm having those kinds of thoughts. I know what that looks like, obviously, from my clients at the clinic. People carry so much guilt, especially with substance abuse stuff. I had this one woman years ago who felt so incredibly guilty about stealing her roommate's toaster to pawn it. \n\nWhich is such a strange thing to pawn, if you think about it, a toaster. It was one of those really nice retro-looking ones, though, the kind that cost like a hundred dollars at those fancy kitchen stores. I actually love walking through those stores just to look at the gadgets. They have a specific tool for absolutely everything now, like an avocado slicer. Why do you need a special plastic tool to slice an avocado? You just use a knife. My husband bought an avocado slicer once, and it just sat in the kitchen drawer getting jammed every time you tried to open it. The drawer in our kitchen always sticks anyway because the wood swelled up last summer when the humidity was so awful. We tried sanding it down to make it fit better, but the sawdust got absolutely everywhere. It took me three days to get it out of the crevices of the blender. And you know how hard blenders are to clean anyway, with the blades at the bottom...\n\nSorry, I completely lost my train of thought again. Guilt, you said. No, I don't feel worthless or guilty. I'm not really having thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about death?\n\nPatient: Death? Um, no. I don't really think about dying or anything like that. I mean, I see enough of that kind of heavy stuff at work, you know? The clinic is right next to this old cemetery, actually. It's one of those really beautiful, historic ones with the big weeping willows and the elaborate stone angels. \n\nI used to walk through there on my lunch break sometimes, just because it was quiet. It's amazing how peaceful it can be right in the middle of the city. There's this one headstone from the 1800s that always caught my eye because the carving on it was of a little ship. I always wondered if the person was a sailor or if it was just symbolic, you know? Like sailing into the next life. My grandfather was in the Navy, actually. He used to tell these incredible stories about being stationed in the Pacific. He brought back this beautiful carved wooden box that my grandmother kept her sewing supplies in. I still have it. It smells like old thread and cedar. Cedar is such a distinct smell, isn't it? We tried to put cedar blocks in our closets last year to keep the moths away, but I swear the moths just ignored them. I ended up finding holes in my favorite wool sweater anyway. It was this gorgeous forest green color that I bought at a little boutique when we went to Vermont. Vermont is so stunning in the fall...\n\nWait, sorry. Death. No, I don't think about death. I just... I don't really have those kinds of thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've lost interest in almost all activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Lost interest... yeah. I guess that's actually what finally brought me in. It's like the part of me that used to reach out for people and actually take pleasure in things has just gone very quiet. I used to love my work, doing yoga, going to art galleries, even just journaling in the evening. \n\nI have this really beautiful leather-bound journal I bought at this little stationery shop downtown. They sell the most amazing pens there, too. You know, those fountain pens that make you feel like you're writing a letter in the nineteenth century? I bought one with this deep blue ink, almost like a sapphire color. It reminds me of the ocean. My husband and I went to the coast for our honeymoon, and the water was exactly that color. We stayed in this tiny bed and breakfast where the owner made the most incredible blueberry scones every morning. I've tried to recreate that recipe so many times, but they never turn out quite right. I think it's because she used fresh buttermilk from a local farm. You just can't get buttermilk like that at the regular grocery store. The grocery store near us is always so chaotic anyway, especially on Sunday afternoons. People just abandon their carts right in the middle of the aisle, and the wheels always squeak...\n\nUm, but yeah. The yoga and the galleries and all of it. I just don't care about them anymore. I'm mostly just moving through obligations now. It's all just very quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your loss of interest in activities is affecting your daily life a lot?\n\nPatient: Affecting my daily life? Yeah, I suppose it is. I mean, I'm still showing up to work at the clinic every day, but it's... it's not the same. I've actually missed a couple of important grant deadlines recently, which is so unlike me. And my supervisor, she's been so nice about it, but she's quietly moved some of my more demanding clients to other clinicians. She can tell I'm just not functioning at my usual level. \n\nShe's a really observant supervisor, actually. She always notices little things, like if someone changes their hair or gets new glasses. I got new glasses a few months ago, these tortoiseshell frames. I've always loved tortoiseshell. It goes with almost everything, you know? Unlike those bright red frames my sister bought last year. She thought they made her look artsy, but they just clashed with all her clothes. She wears a lot of mustard yellow, which is a really hard color to pull off unless you have the right skin tone. We were trying to find her a dress for our cousin's wedding, and she insisted on this yellow silk thing. The wedding was outdoors in August, and silk is just terrible in the heat. It shows every drop of sweat. Plus, there were mosquitoes everywhere. They had those citronella candles burning, but the smell of citronella always gives me a headache. It's so overpowering. I prefer lavender or maybe eucalyptus. I used to hang fresh eucalyptus in my shower because the steam makes it smell like a spa...\n\nSorry, what was I saying? Oh, right. Work. Yeah, I'm just not functioning the way I used to, so it's definitely affecting things.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have lost interest in almost all activities or is it more like most of them?\n\nPatient: Almost all or most... um, I'm not really sure how to measure the difference, to be honest. I haven't really sat down and counted them up or anything. It just feels like... a lot of them. \n\nLike I mentioned the yoga before. I used to go to this studio over on 4th street, and they had these heated bamboo floors that were just incredible in the winter. The winters here get so brutally cold, don't they? I actually had to buy special snow tires for my Subaru last year because the city plows never seem to make it down our street until noon. They really need to update their plowing routes. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he actually bought his own snowblower just so he could do the sidewalks for the whole block. He's such a sweet older man. He used to be a baker, and sometimes he brings over these little tins of shortbread cookies during the holidays. Shortbread is so rich and buttery, you really need a good cup of Earl Grey tea to go with it to cut through that richness. I have this beautiful ceramic teapot that keeps the tea warm for hours. It has these little hand-painted bluebirds on it... \n\nAnyway, sorry. Um, all or most? Yeah, I really couldn't tell you the exact ratio. Just... the things I used to do, I don't really do them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to find some pleasure in at least a few things, even if it's not as much as you used to?\n\nPatient: Find pleasure in a few things... I don't know. Like I was trying to say earlier, it's really just that the part of me that used to reach out for people and actually take pleasure in things—my work at the clinic, the yoga, the art galleries, my journaling—it's all just gone very quiet. I'm mostly just moving through obligations now. \n\nObligations... there are just so many of them, aren't there? I feel like I'm always writing lists. The fridge is stainless steel, which looks nice, but it shows every single fingerprint. My husband is terrible about grabbing the door with messy hands, especially when he's making his famous chili. He uses three different kinds of beans in it. Black beans, kidney beans, and pinto beans. I always tell him that's too many beans, it messes with the texture, but he swears by it. He won a chili cook-off at his office five years ago and hasn't let anyone forget it. The trophy was this ridiculous plastic chili pepper wearing a cowboy hat. We had it on the mantle for a while, right next to this beautiful antique clock my grandmother left me. She bought that clock in Switzerland when she was traveling through Europe in her twenties. She took a train through the Alps, and she used to tell me stories about how the mountains looked like they were carved out of sugar...\n\nBut, um... pleasure. No, not really. Even the few things, it's just... quiet. Just obligations.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that there's anything at all that you still look forward to or enjoy, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Look forward to? Um... Like you're forced to write in straight lines, but sometimes you want to draw a little sketch or make a mind map. I tried a bullet journal once, but keeping up with the index was exhausting. My sister is obsessed with bullet journaling. She has all these expensive mildliner highlighters in pastel colors. She color-codes everything—her meals, her workouts, her moods. I think she spends more time tracking her life than actually living it. She tried to get me into it and bought me this set of pens for my birthday, but they bleed through the paper. There's nothing worse than ink bleeding through to the other side of the page, it completely ruins it. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, looking forward to things... no. I'm mostly just moving through obligations now. It's all just... quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience any pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, unable to experience pleasure at all... I kept thinking it sounded like a tiny airplane trying to take off from my dresser. But then, after a while, I got so used to it that I actually couldn't sleep without it. I went to three different stores because I wanted the exact same model. The new ones have these bright blue LED lights on them that you can't turn off, which is terrible for your circadian rhythm. You're supposed to sleep in complete darkness, you know. My bedroom has blackout curtains, the really thick velvet ones. Interest in activities... I mean, I feel like a broken record here, but it's really the same thing. What finally brought me in is that I just feel like the part of me that used to reach out for people, the part that actually took pleasure in things—my work as a social worker, the yoga classes I used to go to, visiting art galleries, even my journaling—it's all just gone very quiet. 

I used to love my yoga classes. I went to this studio downtown, right above a bakery. The smell of fresh bread would waft up during savasana, which you'd think would be distracting, but it was actually really grounding. Sourdough, mostly. The baker there, this guy named Mateo, he cultivated his own sourdough starter for seven years. He called it 'The Beast' because it was so active. He had to feed it twice a day. It's fascinating how yeast works, isn't it? Just this living organism that you keep in a jar on your counter. My neighbor tried to give me some of her starter once, but I accidentally killed it within a week. I left it near the stove and it got too hot. She was so disappointed in me, she had brought it over in this vintage mason jar with a zinc lid. Those lids are so hard to find now, the ones with the white glass insert. You have to scour antique malls for them. I love antique malls, the way they smell like old paper and dust... 

But, um, interest. No, not really. Like I said, it's all just gone quiet. I'm mostly just moving through obligations.

I actually started looking into composting our paper towels and junk mail to do my part, but you have to be really careful about the glossy grocery store inserts because of the toxic ink. Everything is just plastic that snaps if you look at it wrong. I tried to tape the cover back on, but it was that really cheap, flimsy tape I got at the dollar store. It's just inflation, I know, but it feels a little deceptive. My husband used to love going in there just to see what weird off-brand snacks they had. Just... eating whatever's around.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Moving more slowly? Um, no, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed feeling physically slower or anything. I mean, my morning walk to the subway takes the exact same amount of time it always has. I time it sometimes because I like to listen to this one specific podcast on the way, and the episodes are exactly twenty-two minutes long. \n\nIt's a podcast about historical mysteries, actually. The host has this really soothing voice, almost like a late-night radio DJ. I used to listen to the radio all the time when I was younger, especially when I was driving back and forth to my first social work internship. I had this beat-up old Honda Civic, and the tape deck was broken, so I was stuck with whatever was on the FM dial. You know, they just don't make cars like that anymore. That thing ran forever, even though the heat barely worked. I remember driving in January with my winter coat and gloves on, just freezing. The winters here used to be so much worse, don't you think? Now it barely snows before January, but back then we'd have a foot of snow on the ground by Thanksgiving. I always loved Thanksgiving, making the cranberry sauce from scratch... \n\nUm, anyway. Sorry. Moving slowly. No, I haven't noticed that. I'm just kind of going at my normal pace, even if I don't really want to be doing the things I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Worthless or guilty? Um, no, not really. I don't think I'm having those kinds of thoughts. I know what that looks like, obviously, from my clients at the clinic. People carry so much guilt, especially with substance abuse stuff. I had this one woman years ago who felt so incredibly guilty about stealing her roommate's toaster to pawn it. \n\nWhich is such a strange thing to pawn, if you think about it, a toaster. It was one of those really nice retro-looking ones, though, the kind that cost like a hundred dollars at those fancy kitchen stores. I actually love walking through those stores just to look at the gadgets. They have a specific tool for absolutely everything now, like an avocado slicer. Why do you need a special plastic tool to slice an avocado? You just use a knife. My husband bought an avocado slicer once, and it just sat in the kitchen drawer getting jammed every time you tried to open it. The drawer in our kitchen always sticks anyway because the wood swelled up last summer when the humidity was so awful. We tried sanding it down to make it fit better, but the sawdust got absolutely everywhere. It took me three days to get it out of the crevices of the blender. And you know how hard blenders are to clean anyway, with the blades at the bottom...\n\nSorry, I completely lost my train of thought again. Guilt, you said. No, I don't feel worthless or guilty. I'm not really having thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about death?\n\nPatient: Death? Um, no. I don't really think about dying or anything like that. I mean, I see enough of that kind of heavy stuff at work, you know? The clinic is right next to this old cemetery, actually. It's one of those really beautiful, historic ones with the big weeping willows and the elaborate stone angels. \n\nI used to walk through there on my lunch break sometimes, just because it was quiet. It's amazing how peaceful it can be right in the middle of the city. There's this one headstone from the 1800s that always caught my eye because the carving on it was of a little ship. I always wondered if the person was a sailor or if it was just symbolic, you know? Like sailing into the next life. My grandfather was in the Navy, actually. He used to tell these incredible stories about being stationed in the Pacific. He brought back this beautiful carved wooden box that my grandmother kept her sewing supplies in. I still have it. It smells like old thread and cedar. Cedar is such a distinct smell, isn't it? We tried to put cedar blocks in our closets last year to keep the moths away, but I swear the moths just ignored them. I ended up finding holes in my favorite wool sweater anyway. It was this gorgeous forest green color that I bought at a little boutique when we went to Vermont. Vermont is so stunning in the fall...\n\nWait, sorry. Death. No, I don't think about death. I just... I don't really have those kinds of thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've lost interest in almost all activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Lost interest... yeah. I guess that's actually what finally brought me in. It's like the part of me that used to reach out for people and actually take pleasure in things has just gone very quiet. I used to love my work, doing yoga, going to art galleries, even just journaling in the evening. \n\nI have this really beautiful leather-bound journal I bought at this little stationery shop downtown. They sell the most amazing pens there, too. You know, those fountain pens that make you feel like you're writing a letter in the nineteenth century? I bought one with this deep blue ink, almost like a sapphire color. It reminds me of the ocean. My husband and I went to the coast for our honeymoon, and the water was exactly that color. We stayed in this tiny bed and breakfast where the owner made the most incredible blueberry scones every morning. I've tried to recreate that recipe so many times, but they never turn out quite right. I think it's because she used fresh buttermilk from a local farm. You just can't get buttermilk like that at the regular grocery store. The grocery store near us is always so chaotic anyway, especially on Sunday afternoons. People just abandon their carts right in the middle of the aisle, and the wheels always squeak...\n\nUm, but yeah. The yoga and the galleries and all of it. I just don't care about them anymore. I'm mostly just moving through obligations now. It's all just very quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your loss of interest in activities is affecting your daily life a lot?\n\nPatient: Affecting my daily life? Yeah, I suppose it is. I mean, I'm still showing up to work at the clinic every day, but it's... it's not the same. I've actually missed a couple of important grant deadlines recently, which is so unlike me. And my supervisor, she's been so nice about it, but she's quietly moved some of my more demanding clients to other clinicians. She can tell I'm just not functioning at my usual level. \n\nShe's a really observant supervisor, actually. She always notices little things, like if someone changes their hair or gets new glasses. I got new glasses a few months ago, these tortoiseshell frames. I've always loved tortoiseshell. It goes with almost everything, you know? Unlike those bright red frames my sister bought last year. She thought they made her look artsy, but they just clashed with all her clothes. She wears a lot of mustard yellow, which is a really hard color to pull off unless you have the right skin tone. We were trying to find her a dress for our cousin's wedding, and she insisted on this yellow silk thing. The wedding was outdoors in August, and silk is just terrible in the heat. It shows every drop of sweat. Plus, there were mosquitoes everywhere. They had those citronella candles burning, but the smell of citronella always gives me a headache. It's so overpowering. I prefer lavender or maybe eucalyptus. I used to hang fresh eucalyptus in my shower because the steam makes it smell like a spa...\n\nSorry, what was I saying? Oh, right. Work. Yeah, I'm just not functioning the way I used to, so it's definitely affecting things.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have lost interest in almost all activities or is it more like most of them?\n\nPatient: Almost all or most... um, I'm not really sure how to measure the difference, to be honest. I haven't really sat down and counted them up or anything. It just feels like... a lot of them. \n\nLike I mentioned the yoga before. I used to go to this studio over on 4th street, and they had these heated bamboo floors that were just incredible in the winter. The winters here get so brutally cold, don't they? I actually had to buy special snow tires for my Subaru last year because the city plows never seem to make it down our street until noon. They really need to update their plowing routes. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he actually bought his own snowblower just so he could do the sidewalks for the whole block. He's such a sweet older man. He used to be a baker, and sometimes he brings over these little tins of shortbread cookies during the holidays. Shortbread is so rich and buttery, you really need a good cup of Earl Grey tea to go with it to cut through that richness. I have this beautiful ceramic teapot that keeps the tea warm for hours. It has these little hand-painted bluebirds on it... \n\nAnyway, sorry. Um, all or most? Yeah, I really couldn't tell you the exact ratio. Just... the things I used to do, I don't really do them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to find some pleasure in at least a few things, even if it's not as much as you used to?\n\nPatient: Find pleasure in a few things... I don't know. I'm mostly just moving through obligations now. \n\nObligations... there are just so many of them, aren't there? I feel like I'm always writing lists. The fridge is stainless steel, which looks nice, but it shows every single fingerprint. My husband is terrible about grabbing the door with messy hands, especially when he's making his famous chili. He uses three different kinds of beans in it. Black beans, kidney beans, and pinto beans. I always tell him that's too many beans, it messes with the texture, but he swears by it. He won a chili cook-off at his office five years ago and hasn't let anyone forget it. The trophy was this ridiculous plastic chili pepper wearing a cowboy hat. We had it on the mantle for a while, right next to this beautiful antique clock my grandmother left me. She bought that clock in Switzerland when she was traveling through Europe in her twenties. She took a train through the Alps, and she used to tell me stories about how the mountains looked like they were carved out of sugar...\n\nBut, um... pleasure. No, not really. I kept thinking it sounded like a tiny airplane trying to take off from my dresser. But then, after a while, I got so used to it that I actually couldn't sleep without it. It has these little hand-painted bluebirds on it... \n\nAnyway, sorry. Um, all or most? Yeah, I really couldn't tell you the exact ratio. Just... the things I used to do, I don't really do them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to find some pleasure in at least a few things, even if it's not as much as you used to?\n\nPatient: Find pleasure in a few things... I'm mostly just moving through obligations now. \n\nObligations... there are just so many of them, aren't there? I feel like I'm always writing lists. I have this notepad on my fridge with a magnetic back, but the magnet is really weak, so it's always sliding down and getting caught behind the handle. The fridge is stainless steel, which looks nice, but it shows every single fingerprint. My husband is terrible about grabbing the door with messy hands, especially when he's making his famous chili. He uses three different kinds of beans in it. Black beans, kidney beans, and pinto beans. I always tell him that's too many beans, it messes with the texture, but he swears by it. He won a chili cook-off at his office five years ago and hasn't let anyone forget it. The trophy was this ridiculous plastic chili pepper wearing a cowboy hat. We had it on the mantle for a while, right next to this beautiful antique clock my grandmother left me. She bought that clock in Switzerland when she was traveling through Europe in her twenties. She took a train through the Alps, and she used to tell me stories about how the mountains looked like they were carved out of sugar...\n\nBut, um... pleasure. No, not really. Even the few things, it's just... quiet. Just obligations.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that there's anything at all that you still look forward to or enjoy, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Look forward to? Um... I only buy the ones with the dotted pages because the lined ones feel too restrictive, you know? Like you're forced to write in straight lines, but sometimes you want to draw a little sketch or make a mind map. I tried a bullet journal once, but keeping up with the index was exhausting. My sister is obsessed with bullet journaling. She has all these expensive mildliner highlighters in pastel colors. She color-codes everything—her meals, her workouts, her moods. I think she spends more time tracking her life than actually living it. She tried to get me into it and bought me this set of pens for my birthday, but they bleed through the paper. There's nothing worse than ink bleeding through to the other side of the page, it completely ruins it. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, looking forward to things... no. I'm mostly just moving through obligations now. It's all just... quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience any pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, unable to experience pleasure at all... I kept thinking it sounded like a tiny airplane trying to take off from my dresser. But then, after a while, I got so used to it that I actually couldn't sleep without it. Lost interest in almost everything... yeah. I feel like I'm just repeating myself, but that's really the core of it. That's why I'm sitting here. I just... I feel like the part of me that used to reach out for people, the part that actually took pleasure in things—my work, the yoga, going to galleries, even my journaling—it's all just gone very quiet.

I don't even really drink wine, it gives me a terrible headache, especially red wine. I read once that it's the sulfites, or maybe the tannins. My brother-in-law is a sommelier, or at least he's studying to be one. He has this massive temperature-controlled wine fridge in his basement that takes up half the wall. It's so loud when the compressor kicks on. He's always trying to get me to taste these obscure vintages from, like, the foothills of somewhere in Italy. He bought these special glasses that are supposed to direct the wine to a specific part of your tongue to maximize the flavor profile. I think it's mostly psychological, honestly. Like how people swear by expensive bottled water, but in blind taste tests, they usually prefer tap water. Our city actually has really good tap water, it won an award a few years ago. They send out this little pamphlet with the water bill every year detailing all the mineral contents...

But, yeah. Lost interest. I'm mostly just moving through obligations now. It's just quiet.

I'm mostly just moving through obligations at this point.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Um, let me think. It's been about eight months. Yeah, eight months. It started right around the time we took my older relative into our apartment. We had to completely clear out the second bedroom for them, which was a huge project because I was using it to store all my old social work textbooks from grad school. \n\nThey are so incredibly heavy, those books. I tried to donate them to the local library, but the librarian told me they don't accept textbooks anymore because the information gets outdated too quickly. Which makes sense, I guess, but it just feels like such a terrible waste to throw out perfectly good hardcover books. I mean, the amount of paper that goes into landfills every year is staggering. I actually started looking into composting our paper towels and junk mail to do my part, but you have to be really careful about the glossy grocery store inserts because of the toxic ink. My neighbor—not the one with the SUV, the other one—tried composting and ended up with a yard full of raccoons. They are surprisingly smart animals, raccoons. They figured out how to unlatch her supposedly animal-proof bins in just two nights...\n\nSorry, I'm rambling again. You asked how long. Right. So, about eight months. That's how long this whole change in how I'm thinking, and working, and connecting with people has been going on. Just... ever since they moved in.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Weight? Um, no, I don't think so. I actually had to buy a new scale a few months ago because the battery cover on my old one broke and the batteries kept falling out. \n\nIt's so frustrating how cheaply things are made now. Everything is just plastic that snaps if you look at it wrong. I tried to tape the cover back on, but it was that really cheap, flimsy tape I got at the dollar store. Have you noticed how dollar stores aren't even really a dollar anymore? They changed the name of the one down the street to like, \"Dollar and Quarter Tree\" or something. It's just inflation, I know, but it feels a little deceptive. My husband used to love going in there just to see what weird off-brand snacks they had. He once found these chips that were supposed to taste like a cheeseburger, and they actually smelled exactly like pickles. It was the strangest thing. I can't stand the smell of pickles, honestly, they just... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, weight. No, I haven't lost any weight. Just... eating whatever's around.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Moving more slowly? Um, no, I don't think so. I time it sometimes because I like to listen to this one specific podcast on the way, and the episodes are exactly twenty-two minutes long. \n\nIt's a podcast about historical mysteries, actually. The host has this really soothing voice, almost like a late-night radio DJ. Sorry. Moving slowly. No, I haven't noticed that. Um, no, not really. I don't think I'm having those kinds of thoughts. I know what that looks like, obviously, from my clients at the clinic. People carry so much guilt, especially with substance abuse stuff. I had this one woman years ago who felt so incredibly guilty about stealing her roommate's toaster to pawn it. \n\nWhich is such a strange thing to pawn, if you think about it, a toaster. It was one of those really nice retro-looking ones, though, the kind that cost like a hundred dollars at those fancy kitchen stores. The drawer in our kitchen always sticks anyway because the wood swelled up last summer when the humidity was so awful. We tried sanding it down to make it fit better, but the sawdust got absolutely everywhere. It took me three days to get it out of the crevices of the blender. And you know how hard blenders are to clean anyway, with the blades at the bottom...\n\nSorry, I completely lost my train of thought again. Guilt, you said. No, I don't feel worthless or guilty. I'm not really having thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about death?\n\nPatient: Death? Um, no. I don't really think about dying or anything like that. I mean, I see enough of that kind of heavy stuff at work, you know? The clinic is right next to this old cemetery, actually. It's one of those really beautiful, historic ones with the big weeping willows and the elaborate stone angels. \n\nI used to walk through there on my lunch break sometimes, just because it was quiet. It's amazing how peaceful it can be right in the middle of the city. There's this one headstone from the 1800s that always caught my eye because the carving on it was of a little ship. I always wondered if the person was a sailor or if it was just symbolic, you know? Like sailing into the next life. My grandfather was in the Navy, actually. He used to tell these incredible stories about being stationed in the Pacific. He brought back this beautiful carved wooden box that my grandmother kept her sewing supplies in. I still have it. I ended up finding holes in my favorite wool sweater anyway. It was this gorgeous forest green color that I bought at a little boutique when we went to Vermont. Vermont is so stunning in the fall...\n\nWait, sorry. Death. No, I don't think about death. I just... I don't really have those kinds of thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've lost interest in almost all activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Lost interest... yeah. I guess that's actually what finally brought me in. It's like the part of me that used to reach out for people and actually take pleasure in things has just gone very quiet. I used to love my work, doing yoga, going to art galleries, even just journaling in the evening. \n\nI have this really beautiful leather-bound journal I bought at this little stationery shop downtown. They sell the most amazing pens there, too. You know, those fountain pens that make you feel like you're writing a letter in the nineteenth century? I bought one with this deep blue ink, almost like a sapphire color. It reminds me of the ocean. My husband and I went to the coast for our honeymoon, and the water was exactly that color. We stayed in this tiny bed and breakfast where the owner made the most incredible blueberry scones every morning. I've tried to recreate that recipe so many times, but they never turn out quite right. I think it's because she used fresh buttermilk from a local farm. I'm mostly just moving through obligations now. I mean, I'm still showing up to work at the clinic every day, but it's... it's not the same. I've actually missed a couple of important grant deadlines recently, which is so unlike me. And my supervisor, she's been so nice about it, but she's quietly moved some of my more demanding clients to other clinicians. She can tell I'm just not functioning at my usual level. \n\nShe's a really observant supervisor, actually. She always notices little things, like if someone changes their hair or gets new glasses. Yeah, I'm just not functioning the way I used to, so it's definitely affecting things.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have lost interest in almost all activities or is it more like most of them?\n\nPatient: Almost all or most... um, I'm not really sure how to measure the difference, to be honest. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he actually bought his own snowblower just so he could do the sidewalks for the whole block. He's such a sweet older man. He used to be a baker, and sometimes he brings over these little tins of shortbread cookies during the holidays. It has these little hand-painted bluebirds on it... \n\nAnyway, sorry. Um, all or most? Yeah, I really couldn't tell you the exact ratio. Just... the things I used to do, I don't really do them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to find some pleasure in at least a few things, even if it's not as much as you used to?\n\nPatient: Find pleasure in a few things... My neighbor tried to give me some of her starter once, but I accidentally killed it within a week. I left it near the stove and it got too hot. I always wondered if the person was a sailor or if it was just symbolic, you know? Like sailing into the next life. My grandfather was in the Navy, actually. He used to tell these incredible stories about being stationed in the Pacific. He brought back this beautiful carved wooden box that my grandmother kept her sewing supplies in. I still have it. It smells like old thread and cedar. Cedar is such a distinct smell, isn't it? We tried to put cedar blocks in our closets last year to keep the moths away, but I swear the moths just ignored them. I ended up finding holes in my favorite wool sweater anyway. It was this gorgeous forest green color that I bought at a little boutique when we went to Vermont. Vermont is so stunning in the fall...\n\nWait, sorry. Death. No, I don't think about death. I just... I don't really have those kinds of thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've lost interest in almost all activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Lost interest... yeah. I guess that's actually what finally brought me in. It's like the part of me that used to reach out for people and actually take pleasure in things has just gone very quiet. I used to love my work, doing yoga, going to art galleries, even just journaling in the evening. \n\nI have this really beautiful leather-bound journal I bought at this little stationery shop downtown. They sell the most amazing pens there, too. You know, those fountain pens that make you feel like you're writing a letter in the nineteenth century? I bought one with this deep blue ink, almost like a sapphire color. It reminds me of the ocean. My husband and I went to the coast for our honeymoon, and the water was exactly that color. We stayed in this tiny bed and breakfast where the owner made the most incredible blueberry scones every morning. I've tried to recreate that recipe so many times, but they never turn out quite right. I think it's because she used fresh buttermilk from a local farm. You just can't get buttermilk like that at the regular grocery store. The grocery store near us is always so chaotic anyway, especially on Sunday afternoons. People just abandon their carts right in the middle of the aisle, and the wheels always squeak...\n\nUm, but yeah. The yoga and the galleries and all of it. I just don't care about them anymore. I'm mostly just moving through obligations now. It's all just very quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your loss of interest in activities is affecting your daily life a lot?\n\nPatient: Affecting my daily life? Yeah, I suppose it is. I mean, I'm still showing up to work at the clinic every day, but it's... it's not the same. I've actually missed a couple of important grant deadlines recently, which is so unlike me. And my supervisor, she's been so nice about it, but she's quietly moved some of my more demanding clients to other clinicians. She can tell I'm just not functioning at my usual level. \n\nShe's a really observant supervisor, actually. She always notices little things, like if someone changes their hair or gets new glasses. I got new glasses a few months ago, these tortoiseshell frames. I've always loved tortoiseshell. It goes with almost everything, you know? Unlike those bright red frames my sister bought last year. She thought they made her look artsy, but they just clashed with all her clothes. She wears a lot of mustard yellow, which is a really hard color to pull off unless you have the right skin tone. We were trying to find her a dress for our cousin's wedding, and she insisted on this yellow silk thing. The wedding was outdoors in August, and silk is just terrible in the heat. It shows every drop of sweat. Plus, there were mosquitoes everywhere. They had those citronella candles burning, but the smell of citronella always gives me a headache. It's so overpowering. I prefer lavender or maybe eucalyptus. I used to hang fresh eucalyptus in my shower because the steam makes it smell like a spa...\n\nSorry, what was I saying? Oh, right. Work. Yeah, I'm just not functioning the way I used to, so it's definitely affecting things.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have lost interest in almost all activities or is it more like most of them?\n\nPatient: Almost all or most... um, I'm not really sure how to measure the difference, to be honest. I haven't really sat down and counted them up or anything. It just feels like... a lot of them. \n\nLike I mentioned the yoga before. I used to go to this studio over on 4th street, and they had these heated bamboo floors that were just incredible in the winter. The winters here get so brutally cold, don't they? I actually had to buy special snow tires for my Subaru last year because the city plows never seem to make it down our street until noon. They really need to update their plowing routes. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he actually bought his own snowblower just so he could do the sidewalks for the whole block. He's such a sweet older man. He used to be a baker, and sometimes he brings over these little tins of shortbread cookies during the holidays. Shortbread is so rich and buttery, you really need a good cup of Earl Grey tea to go with it to cut through that richness. I have this beautiful ceramic teapot that keeps the tea warm for hours. It has these little hand-painted bluebirds on it... \n\nAnyway, sorry. Um, all or most? Yeah, I really couldn't tell you the exact ratio. Just... the things I used to do, I don't really do them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to find some pleasure in at least a few things, even if it's not as much as you used to?\n\nPatient: Find pleasure in a few things... I don't know. Like I was trying to say earlier, it's really just that the part of me that used to reach out for people and actually take pleasure in things—my work at the clinic, the yoga, the art galleries, my journaling—it's all just gone very quiet. I'm mostly just moving through obligations now. \n\nObligations... there are just so many of them, aren't there? I feel like I'm always writing lists. I have this notepad on my fridge with a magnetic back, but the magnet is really weak, so it's always sliding down and getting caught behind the handle. The fridge is stainless steel, which looks nice, but it shows every single fingerprint. My husband is terrible about grabbing the door with messy hands, especially when he's making his famous chili. He uses three different kinds of beans in it. Black beans, kidney beans, and pinto beans. I always tell him that's too many beans, it messes with the texture, but he swears by it. He won a chili cook-off at his office five years ago and hasn't let anyone forget it. The trophy was this ridiculous plastic chili pepper wearing a cowboy hat. We had it on the mantle for a while, right next to this beautiful antique clock my grandmother left me. She bought that clock in Switzerland when she was traveling through Europe in her twenties. She took a train through the Alps, and she used to tell me stories about how the mountains looked like they were carved out of sugar...\n\nBut, um... pleasure. No, not really. Even the few things, it's just... quiet. Just obligations.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that there's anything at all that you still look forward to or enjoy, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Look forward to? Um... I guess that's really what finally brought me in to see you. I just... I feel like the part of me that used to reach out for people, the part that actually took pleasure in things, it's just gone very quiet. My work, the yoga, going to art galleries, even my journaling... I used to love journaling. I have this stack of Moleskine notebooks on my nightstand. I only buy the ones with the dotted pages because the lined ones feel too restrictive, you know? Like you're forced to write in straight lines, but sometimes you want to draw a little sketch or make a mind map. I tried a bullet journal once, but keeping up with the index was exhausting. My sister is obsessed with bullet journaling. She has all these expensive mildliner highlighters in pastel colors. She color-codes everything—her meals, her workouts, her moods. I think she spends more time tracking her life than actually living it. She tried to get me into it and bought me this set of pens for my birthday, but they bleed through the paper. There's nothing worse than ink bleeding through to the other side of the page, it completely ruins it. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, looking forward to things... no. I'm mostly just moving through obligations now. It's all just... quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience any pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, unable to experience pleasure at all... I mean, I think I've been saying that, right? That's really what finally brought me in here to see you. It's just that the part of me that used to reach out for people, the part that actually took pleasure in things—my work at the clinic, the yoga, the art galleries, even my journaling—it's all just gone very quiet. \n\nQuiet is such a strange word, isn't it? Like, you think of a quiet room, but even a quiet room has noise. The hum of the refrigerator, or the ticking of a clock, or the sound of your own breathing. I have this humidifier in my bedroom, and it makes this low, constant humming sound. At first, it drove me absolutely crazy. I couldn't sleep for the first three nights I had it. I kept thinking it sounded like a tiny airplane trying to take off from my dresser. But then, after a while, I got so used to it that I actually couldn't sleep without it. When it broke last month—the little motor just burned out, I think because I forgot to clean the filter—I had to go out and buy a new one immediately. I went to three different stores because I wanted the exact same model. The new ones have these bright blue LED lights on them that you can't turn off, which is terrible for your circadian rhythm. You're supposed to sleep in complete darkness, you know. My bedroom has blackout curtains, the really thick velvet ones. They're a deep emerald green, which I read is a very calming color for a bedroom...\n\nBut, um, yes. Pleasure. I'm mostly just moving through obligations now. That's really all it is.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have no interest at all in any activities, or is there still some small interest in a few things?\n\nPatient: Interest in activities... I mean, I feel like a broken record here, but it's really the same thing. What finally brought me in is that I just feel like the part of me that used to reach out for people, the part that actually took pleasure in things—my work as a social worker, the yoga classes I used to go to, visiting art galleries, even my journaling—it's all just gone very quiet. \n\nI used to love my yoga classes. I went to this studio downtown, right above a bakery. The smell of fresh bread would waft up during savasana, which you'd think would be distracting, but it was actually really grounding. Sourdough, mostly. The baker there, this guy named Mateo, he cultivated his own sourdough starter for seven years. He called it 'The Beast' because it was so active. He had to feed it twice a day. It's fascinating how yeast works, isn't it? Just this living organism that you keep in a jar on your counter. My neighbor tried to give me some of her starter once, but I accidentally killed it within a week. I left it near the stove and it got too hot. She was so disappointed in me, she had brought it over in this vintage mason jar with a zinc lid. Those lids are so hard to find now, the ones with the white glass insert. You have to scour antique malls for them. I love antique malls, the way they smell like old paper and dust... \n\nBut, um, interest. No, not really. Like I said, it's all just gone quiet. I'm mostly just moving through obligations.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have lost interest in almost everything you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Lost interest in almost everything... yeah. I feel like I'm just repeating myself, but that's really the core of it. That's why I'm sitting here. I just... I feel like the part of me that used to reach out for people, the part that actually took pleasure in things—my work, the yoga, going to galleries, even my journaling—it's all just gone very quiet. \n\nI used to go to this one gallery on 4th Street every First Friday. They always had this amazing spread of cheese and wine. I don't even really drink wine, it gives me a terrible headache, especially red wine. I read once that it's the sulfites, or maybe the tannins. My brother-in-law is a sommelier, or at least he's studying to be one. He has this massive temperature-controlled wine fridge in his basement that takes up half the wall. It's so loud when the compressor kicks on. He's always trying to get me to taste these obscure vintages from, like, the foothills of somewhere in Italy. He bought these special glasses that are supposed to direct the wine to a specific part of your tongue to maximize the flavor profile. I think it's mostly psychological, honestly. Like how people swear by expensive bottled water, but in blind taste tests, they usually prefer tap water. Our city actually has really good tap water, it won an award a few years ago. They send out this little pamphlet with the water bill every year detailing all the mineral contents...\n\nBut, yeah. Lost interest. I'm mostly just moving through obligations now. It's just quiet.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Lost interest in all activities... I know I keep saying the same thing, but it's just the truth. It's exactly why I made the appointment. I just feel like the part of me that used to reach for people and take pleasure in my work at the clinic, the yoga, going to galleries, even my journaling... it's all just gone very quiet. 

I used to journal every single morning. I had this specific type of pen I had to use, a Japanese gel pen, 0.38 millimeter tip. It writes so finely, you can fit so much on one page. I used to order them in bulk online because you couldn't find that exact size in the office supply stores here. The office supply store near my house actually closed down last month. It's turning into a mattress store. I don't understand how mattress stores stay in business, honestly. There are three of them on that same street now. How often do people buy mattresses? You're supposed to replace them every eight years, I think, but still. My mattress is memory foam, one of those ones that comes rolled up in a box. It's so heavy, the delivery guy left it at the bottom of the stairs and I had to push it up end-over-end by myself. I pulled a muscle in my shoulder doing it, right in the rotator cuff. Shoulders are so complicated, the joint is so shallow, which is why it's so easy to dislocate...

But... yeah. Enjoyment. There really isn't any. I'm mostly just moving through obligations.

Probably still in a box in my brother's basement. He has so much junk down there, he can't even fit his car in the garage...\n\nI'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. You were asking about food? Right. No, I don't really enjoy it anymore. Like I was saying earlier, that whole part of me that used to take pleasure in things—my work, yoga, going to galleries, even writing in my journal—it's just gone quiet. I don't get excited about a good meal anymore. I'm just... eating because I have to, just like I'm doing everything else. Just moving through obligations.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I was actually just saying that a minute ago. It's exactly why I finally came in to see you. The part of me that used to reach out for people, or enjoy my work, or yoga... \n\nI used to have this really wonderful yoga mat, actually. It was made of recycled cork. They say cork is naturally antimicrobial, which is nice, but honestly, it always smelled a little bit like a wine cork. My neighbor loves wine, she actually went to that new vineyard they opened out on Route 9 last weekend. She said the traffic to get out there was absolutely horrendous, though. She sat on the highway for two hours. She drives one of those massive SUVs, which just seems so impractical for living in the city. I don't know how she ever finds street parking. I mean, with those new app meters I was talking about earlier, it's hard enough to park a compact car, let alone a tank like that...\n\nUm, sorry. I lost my place again. You asked about losing interest in things. Yes. Like I said earlier, it's all just gone very quiet. The yoga, the journaling, the galleries... I don't enjoy any of it. I'm mostly just moving through obligations at this point.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Um, let me think. It's been about eight months. Yeah, eight months. It started right around the time we took my older relative into our apartment. We had to completely clear out the second bedroom for them, which was a huge project because I was using it to store all my old social work textbooks from grad school. \n\nThey are so incredibly heavy, those books. I tried to donate them to the local library, but the librarian told me they don't accept textbooks anymore because the information gets outdated too quickly. Which makes sense, I guess, but it just feels like such a terrible waste to throw out perfectly good hardcover books. I mean, the amount of paper that goes into landfills every year is staggering. I actually started looking into composting our paper towels and junk mail to do my part, but you have to be really careful about the glossy grocery store inserts because of the toxic ink. My neighbor—not the one with the SUV, the other one—tried composting and ended up with a yard full of raccoons. They are surprisingly smart animals, raccoons. They figured out how to unlatch her supposedly animal-proof bins in just two nights...\n\nSorry, I'm rambling again. You asked how long. Right. So, about eight months. Just... ever since they moved in.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Weight? Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, my clothes still fit the same as they always have. I haven't really noticed any big changes on the scale or anything. I actually had to buy a new scale a few months ago because the battery cover on my old one broke and the batteries kept falling out. \n\nIt's so frustrating how cheaply things are made now. Everything is just plastic that snaps if you look at it wrong. I tried to tape the cover back on, but it was that really cheap, flimsy tape I got at the dollar store. Have you noticed how dollar stores aren't even really a dollar anymore? They changed the name of the one down the street to like, \"Dollar and Quarter Tree\" or something. It's just inflation, I know, but it feels a little deceptive. My husband used to love going in there just to see what weird off-brand snacks they had. He once found these chips that were supposed to taste like a cheeseburger, and they actually smelled exactly like pickles. It was the strangest thing. I can't stand the smell of pickles, honestly, they just... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, weight. No, I haven't lost any weight. Even with not enjoying food, I guess I'm still eating enough to maintain where I'm at. Just... eating whatever's around.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Moving more slowly? Um, no, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed feeling physically slower or anything. I mean, my morning walk to the subway takes the exact same amount of time it always has. I time it sometimes because I like to listen to this one specific podcast on the way, and the episodes are exactly twenty-two minutes long. \n\nIt's a podcast about historical mysteries, actually. The host has this really soothing voice, almost like a late-night radio DJ. I used to listen to the radio all the time when I was younger, especially when I was driving back and forth to my first social work internship. I had this beat-up old Honda Civic, and the tape deck was broken, so I was stuck with whatever was on the FM dial. You know, they just don't make cars like that anymore. That thing ran forever, even though the heat barely worked. I remember driving in January with my winter coat and gloves on, just freezing. The winters here used to be so much worse, don't you think? Now it barely snows before January, but back then we'd have a foot of snow on the ground by Thanksgiving. I always loved Thanksgiving, making the cranberry sauce from scratch... \n\nUm, anyway. Sorry. Moving slowly. No, I haven't noticed that. I'm just kind of going at my normal pace, even if I don't really want to be doing the things I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Worthless or guilty? Um, no, not really. I don't think I'm having those kinds of thoughts. I know what that looks like, obviously, from my clients at the clinic. People carry so much guilt, especially with substance abuse stuff. I had this one woman years ago who felt so incredibly guilty about stealing her roommate's toaster to pawn it. \n\nWhich is such a strange thing to pawn, if you think about it, a toaster. The drawer in our kitchen always sticks anyway because the wood swelled up last summer when the humidity was so awful. We tried sanding it down to make it fit better, but the sawdust got absolutely everywhere. It took me three days to get it out of the crevices of the blender. And you know how hard blenders are to clean anyway, with the blades at the bottom...\n\nSorry, I completely lost my train of thought again. Guilt, you said. No, I don't feel worthless or guilty. I'm not really having thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about death?\n\nPatient: Death? Um, no. I don't really think about dying or anything like that. I mean, I see enough of that kind of heavy stuff at work, you know? The clinic is right next to this old cemetery, actually. It's one of those really beautiful, historic ones with the big weeping willows and the elaborate stone angels. \n\nI used to walk through there on my lunch break sometimes, just because it was quiet. It's amazing how peaceful it can be right in the middle of the city. There's this one headstone from the 1800s that always caught my eye because the carving on it was of a little ship. I always wondered if the person was a sailor or if it was just symbolic, you know? Like sailing into the next life. My grandfather was in the Navy, actually. He used to tell these incredible stories about being stationed in the Pacific. He brought back this beautiful carved wooden box that my grandmother kept her sewing supplies in. I still have it. It smells like old thread and cedar. Cedar is such a distinct smell, isn't it? We tried to put cedar blocks in our closets last year to keep the moths away, but I swear the moths just ignored them. I ended up finding holes in my favorite wool sweater anyway. It was this gorgeous forest green color that I bought at a little boutique when we went to Vermont. Vermont is so stunning in the fall...\n\nWait, sorry. Death. No, I don't think about death. I just... I don't really have those kinds of thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've lost interest in almost all activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Lost interest... yeah. I guess that's actually what finally brought me in. It's like the part of me that used to reach out for people and actually take pleasure in things has just gone very quiet. I used to love my work, doing yoga, going to art galleries, even just journaling in the evening. \n\nI have this really beautiful leather-bound journal I bought at this little stationery shop downtown. They sell the most amazing pens there, too. You know, those fountain pens that make you feel like you're writing a letter in the nineteenth century? I bought one with this deep blue ink, almost like a sapphire color. It reminds me of the ocean. My husband and I went to the coast for our honeymoon, and the water was exactly that color. We stayed in this tiny bed and breakfast where the owner made the most incredible blueberry scones every morning. I've tried to recreate that recipe so many times, but they never turn out quite right. I think it's because she used fresh buttermilk from a local farm.
